# Fact sheet

Generated from `data/db/history.sqlite` by `scripts/db/export_factsheet.py`. Do not edit: the JSON under `data/` is the source of truth. Dates are historical years (no year 0).

Flags: **UNSOURCED** = the entity cites no source; **SOURCE-ID-UNDEFINED** = it cites ids that no sources file defines; **CONJECTURAL** = evidence class conjectural; **LOW-CONF** = confidence low; `review:` = latest verdict recorded in curated.sqlite.

## England and Wales (`england-wales`)

This map shows the land that became England and Wales. For most of the period neither existed: 'England' as a single kingdom dates only from the 10th century (Aethelstan, 927), and Wales was a land of separate kingdoms until the conquest of 1282-3. Earlier names, peoples and borders are shown as they stood at each date; borders were zones rather than lines, and the outlines drawn are indicative.

1993 facts; 299 unsourced; 11 conjectural.

## Barnet, 1471 (`barnet`)

A landscape reconstruction of about 3 × 4 km from Chipping Barnet north through Hadley Green to Kitts End, built to show the ground of the Battle of Barnet (14 April 1471). The battle certainly happened somewhere along this stretch of road, but its exact position is disputed: the registered battlefield centres on Hadley Green, while the 2015–18 survey could not find the battlefield and judged that the ground around Kitts End fits the written sources better. Both areas are shown as shaded zones, not as a located site. The blocks of figures are abstract stand-ins on one modern interpretation: they do not show real numbers, real positions or individual people.

113 facts; 7 unsourced; 48 conjectural.

| 1471 CE | episode attested: A chapel for the dead was built afterwards on or near the field (Great Chronicle of London, about 1512). | Battle of Barnet (episode) |  |  | bar-barnet-battlefield-project-2015-2018,bar-watson-2022-looking-for-the-site,bar-battlefields-trust-barnet,bar-arrivall-part-3,bar-warkworth-barnet,bar-barnet-society-2021,bar-barnet-museum-battle |  |
| 1471 CE | episode attested: Survey 2015–18: systematic metal-detecting, mainly in Wrotham Park, found no clear battle scatter. A few lead round shot found earlier in the Wrotham Park, Kitts End and Old Fold area are probably from the battle. No mass grave has been found. | Battle of Barnet (episode) |  |  | bar-barnet-battlefield-project-2015-2018,bar-watson-2022-looking-for-the-site,bar-battlefields-trust-barnet,bar-arrivall-part-3,bar-warkworth-barnet,bar-barnet-society-2021,bar-barnet-museum-battle |  |
| 1471 CE | episode tradition: Army sizes. The Arrivall (the king's side) gives 9,000 against 30,000; Warkworth gives Warwick 20,000. Modern figures of about 10,000–12,000 Yorkists against about 15,000 Lancastrians (Battlefields Trust; Barnet Museum gives 10,000–13,000 and 13,000–15,000) are estimates. | Battle of Barnet (episode) |  |  | bar-barnet-battlefield-project-2015-2018,bar-watson-2022-looking-for-the-site,bar-battlefields-trust-barnet,bar-arrivall-part-3,bar-warkworth-barnet,bar-barnet-society-2021,bar-barnet-museum-battle |  |
| 1471 CE | episode tradition: The textbook deployment (Lancastrians: Oxford on the right, Montagu in the centre, Exeter on the left, Warwick in reserve; Yorkists: Hastings left, Edward centre, Gloucester right) is a modern reconstruction, not something a contemporary wrote down. | Battle of Barnet (episode) |  |  | bar-barnet-battlefield-project-2015-2018,bar-watson-2022-looking-for-the-site,bar-battlefields-trust-barnet,bar-arrivall-part-3,bar-warkworth-barnet,bar-barnet-society-2021,bar-barnet-museum-battle |  |

| 1471 CE | episode tradition: Casualties: Warkworth says 4,000; other figures run from about 1,000 to 10,000. ## Brentford and the Thames ford (`brentford`)

About 3 × 2.4 km around the meeting of the River Brent and the Thames, about 15 km west of London Bridge. This is a reconstruction of a landscape, not of a battle. In 1016 the Anglo-Saxon Chronicle says only that King Edmund 'went over at Brentford', fought the Danish army and put it to flight, and that many of the English drowned through their own carelessness, having gone ahead of the army for plunder. Where the crossing was, which bank the fighting was on, how many fought and how the day went are not recorded, and no archaeology of the battle is known. No trace of a late Saxon settlement has been excavated at Brentford either. The river, banks and road are placed from the modern map and the county history; the houses, fields, figures and shaded areas are labelled guesses.

80 facts; 2 unsourced; 36 conjectural.

### Sources cited at Brentford and the Thames ford

- `bre-asc-avalon`: The Anglo-Saxon Chronicle, eleventh century (entries for 1013 and 1016; Ingram's translation) (Avalon Project, Yale Law School) <https://avalon.law.yale.edu/medieval/ang11.asp>
- `bre-aubrey-domesday-hanwell`: Hanwell, Middlesex, Domesday Book 1086 (secondary summary; its household totals are internally inconsistent) (Aubrey Research) <https://www.aubreyresearch.com/domesday/mdx/hanwell>
- `bre-bclhs-brentford-bridge`: The History of Brentford Bridge (Brentford & Chiswick Local History Society) <https://brentfordandchiswicklhs.org.uk/?p=2620>
- `bre-brentfordhistory-to-793`: To 793 (The History of Brentford) (brentfordhistory.com (local history site)) <https://brentfordhistory.com/early-days/to-793/>
- `bre-clerk-siege-london`: The Danish Conquest, Part 10: The Siege of London (Old English text and translation of the 1016 siege passage; Lidsmannaflokkr) (A Clerk of Oxford (academic blog)) <https://aclerkofoxford.blogspot.com/2016/05/the-danish-conquest-part-10-siege-of.html>
- `bre-opendomesday-isleworth`: Isleworth, Middlesex, in Domesday Book (Open Domesday (Anna Powell-Smith; data from the Hull Domesday Project)) <https://opendomesday.org/place/TQ1675/isleworth/>
- `bre-osm-nominatim`: OpenStreetMap (Nominatim search and an Overpass extract, November 2026): modern Thames centreline, aits, Brent course and High Street used to place features (OpenStreetMap contributors) <https://nominatim.openstreetmap.org/search?q=Brentford+Ait&format=json&limit=3>
- `bre-tdp-foreshore-pioneers`: Foreshore pioneers (Riverpedia): Wheeler's 1928 excavation near the Brent mouth and later foreshore work at Brentford (Thames Discovery Programme) <https://old.thamesdiscovery.org/riverpedia/foreshore-pioneers>
- `bre-vch-mdx3-heston-isleworth`: Heston and Isleworth: Introduction, in A History of the County of Middlesex, Volume 3 (Victoria County History, 1962), pp. 85-94 (British History Online) <https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/middx/vol3/pp85-94>
- `bre-vch-mdx7-comms`: Ealing and Brentford: Communications, in A History of the County of Middlesex, Volume 7 (Victoria County History, 1982), pp. 101-105 (British History Online) <https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/middx/vol7/pp101-105>
- `bre-vch-mdx7-econ`: Ealing and Brentford: Economic history (fields, fisheries, mills, osiers, market), in A History of the County of Middlesex, Volume 7 (Victoria County History, 1982), pp. 131-144 (British History Online) <https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/middx/vol7/pp131-144>
- `bre-vch-mdx7-growth`: Ealing and Brentford: Growth of Brentford, in A History of the County of Middlesex, Volume 7 (Victoria County History, 1982), pp. 113-120 (British History Online) <https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/middx/vol7/pp113-120>
- `bre-vch-mdx7-intro`: Ealing and Brentford: Introduction (boundaries, relief, geology, streams), in A History of the County of Middlesex, Volume 7 (Victoria County History, 1982), pp. 100-101 (British History Online) <https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/middx/vol7/pp100-101>
- `bre-vch-surrey3-kew`: Parishes: Kew, in A History of the County of Surrey, Volume 3 (Victoria County History, 1911), pp. 482-487 (British History Online) <https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/surrey/vol3/pp482-487>
- `bre-wheeler-old-england`: 'Old England', Brentford, R. E. M. Wheeler, Antiquity 3(9), 1929, pp. 20-32 (only the publisher's extract was read) (Cambridge University Press) <https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/antiquity/article/old-england-brentford/CB6847588D316CDA1F874D72B94B59AE>
- `bre-wikipedia-battle`: Battle of Brentford (1016) (lead only: used to find the Stenton, Poole and Knutsdrapa readings, none of which was read directly) (Wikipedia) <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Brentford_(1016)>
- `bre-wikipedia-brentford-dock`: Brentford Dock (lead only: the dock stood on an island between the Thames and two mouths of the Brent) (Wikipedia) <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brentford_Dock>
- `bre-wikipedia-syon-house`: Syon House (lead only: the tidal meadow on the river bank, flooded twice a day) (Wikipedia) <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syon_House>

## London and the Thames (`london`)

This is a single anchor location: about 4.8 × 3 km around the City of London, Southwark and Westminster. It is not a visualisation of England as a whole. Most of the country changed on different timetables.

| 60 CE | episode attested: Tacitus (Annals 14.33) is the only ancient writer to name Londinium in this context. | 60 CE | episode attested: Recovery: a stylus tablet from the Bloomberg site records a contract dated 21 October AD 62 to bring twenty loads of provisions from Verulamium to London; a fort of about 3.7 acres with earth-and-timber ramparts and double ditches was built at Plantation Place (Fenchurch Street) in about AD 63. | Boudica's sack of Londinium (episode) |  |  | ev-tacitus-annals-book-xiv-chapters-29-39-loeb-tran,ev-cassius-dio-roman-history-epitome-of-book-lxii-l,ev-introduction-london-in-a-d-60-in-an-inventory-of,ev-a-research-framework-for-london-archaeology-2002,ev-roman-fort-built-in-response-to-boudica-s-uprisi,ev-bloomberg-writing-tablets-contract-of-21-october,ev-letters-from-londinium,ev-ad-50-the-story-of-roman-london,ev-london-s-catastrophic-fires,ev-boudica-s-revolt-and-the-sack-of-london-secondar |  |
| 60 CE | episode tradition: Casualties: Tacitus gives 'close upon seventy thousand' Roman citizens and allies killed across the places attacked (Camulodunum, Londinium, Verulamium together, not London alone). Cassius Dio gives 80,000 and 'two cities' sacked, without naming London. Both are rhetorical round numbers; the Loeb editor's note on Tacitus calls the figure probably valueless. | Boudica's sack of Londinium (episode) |  |  | ev-tacitus-annals-book-xiv-chapters-29-39-loeb-tran,ev-cassius-dio-roman-history-epitome-of-book-lxii-l,ev-introduction-london-in-a-d-60-in-an-inventory-of,ev-a-research-framework-for-london-archaeology-2002,ev-roman-fort-built-in-response-to-boudica-s-uprisi,ev-bloomberg-writing-tablets-contract-of-21-october,ev-letters-from-londinium,ev-ad-50-the-story-of-roman-london,ev-london-s-catastrophic-fires,ev-boudica-s-revolt-and-the-sack-of-london-secondar |  |
| 60 CE | episode tradition: Atrocities: Dio's detailed account of mutilation and impalement of captives is not tied to London and has no archaeological support. | 1014 CE | episode attested: Near-contemporary skaldic verse exists: Ottarr svarti's Hofudlausn, stanza 7, addresses Olaf with 'you broke the bryggjur of London'; Sigvatr Thordarson's Vikingarvisur lists a battle of Olaf's at London and Sudvirki (Southwark). | 1014 CE | episode tradition: The whole narrative of the attack comes from Snorri's Heimskringla (Saga of St Olaf, written c. 1230): Danes holding London and a fortification at Southwark with large ditches and a rampart of timber, stone and turf; a bridge wide enough for two wagons to pass, with towers and breast-high wooden parapets on the downstream side, standing on piles. | Battle of London Bridge (episode) |  |  | ev-fact-or-folklore-the-viking-attack-on-london-bri,ev-heimskringla-saga-of-olaf-haraldson-chapter-on-t,ev-olaf-haraldsson-encyclopaedia-romana,ev-the-anglo-saxon-chronicle-eleventh-century-entri,ev-tooley-street-delight-19-march-2022-sigvatr-s-st |  |
| 1014 CE | episode tradition: The tactics are Snorri's alone: Olaf roofed his ships with platforms made from pulled-down houses (wood bound with hazel or withies, often rendered 'wattle' or 'wickerwork' in modern retellings) on stout posts, rowed up under the bridge under a hail of stones and missiles, fastened cables round the piles, and rowed hard downstream until the piles loosened and the loaded bridge gave way; Southwark was then stormed and London submitted to Aethelred. | Battle of London Bridge (episode) |  |  | ev-fact-or-folklore-the-viking-attack-on-london-bri,ev-heimskringla-saga-of-olaf-haraldson-chapter-on-t,ev-olaf-haraldsson-encyclopaedia-romana,ev-the-anglo-saxon-chronicle-eleventh-century-entri,ev-tooley-street-delight-19-march-2022-sigvatr-s-st |  |
| 1014 CE | episode tradition: That 'bryggjur' means the bridge: the word is plural and can mean wharves or jetties. | 1471 CE | episode attested: Misaligned lines: the Arrivall says the two battles did not meet 'directly frount to frount' because of the mist. | 1471 CE | episode attested: Warwick had arrived first and chose the ground; the Arrivall has him 'undre an hedge-syde' somewhat more than half a mile from Barnet; von Wesel and the Great Chronicle of London imply nearer a mile. | 1471 CE | episode tradition: Army sizes: the sources contradict each other along party lines. Arrivall (Yorkist): 9,000 against 30,000. Warkworth: Edward about 7,000-8,000 against Warwick 20,000. Von Wesel: Edward 15,000-20,000. Polydore Vergil (decades later): Edward 'far superior' in numbers. Modern compromise figures (Hammond: about 12,000 Yorkists, 15,000 Lancastrians; others 10,000 and 15,000) are estimates, and the project report notes historians now tend to reduce such numbers further. | Battle of Barnet (episode) |  |  | ev-the-barnet-battlefield-project-2015-2018-g-foard,ev-looking-for-the-site-of-the-battle-of-barnet-b-w,ev-mystery-remains-over-lost-battlefield-site-of-14,ev-warkworth-s-chronicle-the-battle-of-barnet-text-,ev-how-much-ancient-divisions-survive-unnatural-all,ev-the-battle-of-barnet-and-the-great-north-road-se |  |
| 1471 CE | episode tradition: The tidy textbook deployment (Lancastrians: Oxford right, Montagu centre, Exeter left, Warwick in reserve; Yorkists: Hastings left, Edward centre, Gloucester right) is a modern reconstruction. The wing positions are inferred from military convention (vanguard on the right) plus the Arrivall; Hall, the only chronicler to give a Lancastrian order, puts Oxford and Montagu on the left, Warwick and Exeter on the right, and Somerset (who was not there) in the centre. | Battle of Barnet (episode) |  |  | ev-the-barnet-battlefield-project-2015-2018-g-foard,ev-looking-for-the-site-of-the-battle-of-barnet-b-w,ev-mystery-remains-over-lost-battlefield-site-of-14,ev-warkworth-s-chronicle-the-battle-of-barnet-text-,ev-how-much-ancient-divisions-survive-unnatural-all,ev-the-battle-of-barnet-and-the-great-north-road-se |  |

## Stonehenge and its landscape (`stonehenge`)

A 6.4 by 5 km piece of the Stonehenge World Heritage landscape, from 4000 BCE to 1625 CE, with the sarsen circle at the origin. Two levels of detail: the whole landscape is a schematic overview (barrows, cursus, henges, hillfort and town are placed from mapped outlines and published dates, but their form is generic), while the 'district' round the stones (about 800 by 650 m) has stone-by-stone megaliths from a catalogue, the ditch and bank, the Aubrey Holes, the first stretch of the Avenue and the Heel Stone. No feature is shown before its date, and where a date is a range or a model the landmark says so. The state of the stones before the first drawings (de Heere, 1570s) is mostly undated: which stones stood, leant or lay in 1625 is inferred, not recorded. Height comes from a modern LIDAR surface with modern works removed, with the ditch and Avenue ditches cut back in on their dates. Ground cover, trees and house positions are reconstruction. The solstice episodes mark the sunrise and sunset directions with overlay lines and discs; the scene's one fixed sun is not moved to the solstice. Amesbury's outline and the Durrington settlement area are schematic envelopes, not surveyed boundaries.

651 facts; 7 unsourced; 65 conjectural.
